class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span><span class="arraycolsep"></span></span></span><span class="mclose delimcenter"><span class="delimsizing size3">]</span></span></span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel"><span class="mrel"><span class="mord"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="rlap"><span class="strut"></span><span class="inner"><span class="mrel"></span></span><span class="fix"></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mrel">=</span></span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> It’s just an unfortunate notational accident that <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> looks as though it should be the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span> vector. Fortunately, in practice this distinction is easy to get used to, and doesn’t cause confusion. But it’s worth noting.</p><div class="review_set" id="reviewSet-3"><div class="ReviewArea" touch-action="manipulation"><div class="BottomControlArea"><div class="ReviewButtonArea"><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 31 31" fill="none"><path d="M15.5 7C10.2533 7 6 11.2533 6 16.5C6 21.7467 10.2533 26 15.5 26C20.7467 26 25 21.7467 25 16.5C25 15.3037 24.7789 14.159 24.3752 13.1046" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round"></path><path d="M15 11.5V2.5L22 7L15 11.5Z"></path></svg>Didn’t remember</button><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 33 33" fill="none"><path d="M5.99981 18.5564L13.778 26.3345L26.7416 8.18545" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><h2 id="what-does-the-quantum-state-mean">What does the quantum state mean? Why is it a vector in a complex vector space?</h2><blockquote class="Flavor">My own conclusion is that today there is no interpretation of quantum mechanics that does not have serious flaws. This view is not universally shared. Indeed, many physicists are satisfied with their own interpretation of quantum mechanics. But different physicists are satisfied with different interpretations.<p class="Attribution">— Steven Weinberg,<br>Nobel Laureate in Physics</p></blockquote><p>Let’s come back to the question of what the quantum state means, and why it’s a vector in a complex vector space.</p><p>In the case of classical bits, it’s pretty easy to think about the state. You can think of the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span> or <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span> states of a bit as corresponding to two very different (but stable) states of a physical system. For instance, a <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span> or <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span> can be indicated by the presence or absence of a hole at some location in a punch card. And so a single punch card can be used to store hundreds or thousands of bits:</p><img src="/assets/punchcard.jpg" class="FullBleed"><p class="ImageCaption">Source: <a href="https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Blue-punch-card-front.png">Gwern</a> (2006).</p><p>Most ways of storing classical bits are variations on this idea. For instance, the dynamic random access memory (RAM) inside your computer is based on the idea of having two tiny metal plates separated by a miniscule gap. Electric charge is stored on those plates, setting up an electric field between them. The <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span> states of the bit correspond to two different configurations of charge on the plates. In practice, real dynamic RAM systems use slightly more elaborate ideas, but that’s the heart of it. This is harder to think about than punch cards – most of us don’t have so much experience thinking about moving electric charges around metal plates. But it’s still pretty concrete.</p><p>So, how should we think about quantum states? The <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> states often aren’t difficult to think about, since they often correspond to very concrete physical states of the world, much like classical bits. Indeed, in some proposals they may correspond to different charge configurations, similar to dynamic RAM. Or perhaps a photon being in one of two different locations in space – again, a pretty simple, concrete notion, even if photons aren’t that familiar. There are also many more exotic proposals for the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> states. But by and large you can think of the computational basis states as representing a physical system in one of two well-defined configurations.</p><p>Of course, qubits have states which aren’t computational basis states, states like <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0.6</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mn>0.8</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.6|0\rangle+0.8|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">.</span><span class="mord">6</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">.</span><span class="mord">8</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> How should we think about such superposition states? At least in popular media accounts, a very common description is that a state like <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0.6</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mn>0.8</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.6|0\rangle + 0.8|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">.</span><span class="mord">6</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">.</span><span class="mord">8</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> is <em>“simultaneously”</em> in the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> state and the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> state.</p><p>I must confess, I don’t understand what people mean by this. As far as I can tell, what they’re trying to do is explain the quantum state in terms of classical concepts they’re already familiar with. But I can’t make much sense of it – saying <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0.6</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mn>0.8</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.6|0\rangle + 0.8|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">.</span><span class="mord">6</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">.</span><span class="mord">8</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> is <em>simultaneously</em> in the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> state and the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> state seems like word salad, and makes about as much sense to me as Lewis Carroll’s nonsense poem <em>Jabberwocky</em>:</p><blockquote>’Twas brillig, and the slithy toves<br>     Did gyre and gimble in the wabe:<br>All mimsy were the borogoves,<br>    And the mome raths outgrabe.<br>…</blockquote><p>Of course, lacking any other interpretation it’s tempting to try to impose our classical prejudices on the quantum state. Or, even if you reject that, to get hung up worrying about what a quantum state is. But the trouble is that there is enormous disagreement amongst physicists themselves about how to think about the quantum state. Indeed, many active researchers are trying to understand what the correct way of thinking about the quantum state is, exploring multiple approaches in great depth. So we’re going to defer worrying too much about this until later.</p><p>To understand why we’re deferring, suppose someone gives you a Rubik’s cube (or some other challenging puzzle or game) for the first time, all scrambled up. You start to theorize about the best ways of solving it, how to understand it, and so on. But you never actually play around with it, getting familiar with how it behaves.</p><p>Often the best way to understand something is to first use it, to get comfortable, to play a lot, and to do lots of informal experiments. As you build familiarity you understand why things are the way they are. At that point, you can go back and better understand the meaning of the basics.</p><p>Well, we can think of quantum computing and quantum mechanics as an especially complicated type of puzzle! So the strategy we’re taking is to start with the mathematics of quantum computing – we’ll keep getting familiar with qubits and the quantum state, and developing the consequences. Doing that is how we’ll build up intuition, and will give us the chops needed to come back and think harder about the meaning of the quantum state.</p><h1 id="part-II">Part II: Introducing quantum logic gates</h1><p>In Part I we learned about the state of a qubit. But in order to quantum compute, it’s not enough just to understand quantum states. We need to be able to do things with them! We do that using <em>quantum logic gates</em>.</p><p>A quantum logic gate is simply a way of manipulating quantum information, that is, the quantum state of a qubit or a collection of qubits. They’re analogous to the classical logic gates used in ordinary, everyday computers – gates such as the AND, OR, and NOT gates. And, much like classical gates’ role in conventional computers, quantum gates are the basic building blocks of quantum computation. They’re also a convenient way of describing many other quantum information processing tasks, such as quantum teleportation.</p><p>In Part II of this essay we’ll discuss several types of quantum logic gate. As we’ll see, the gates we discuss are sufficient to do any quantum computation. In particular, much as any classical computation can be built up using AND, OR, and NOT gates, the quantum gates we describe over the next few sections suffice to do any quantum computation.</p><p>Many of the quantum gates we’ll learn about are based on familiar classical logic gates. But a few are different. Those differences appear innocuous, almost trivial. But in those differences lies the power of quantum computation, and the possibility for quantum computers to be vastly superior to classical computers.</p><p>Part II is, frankly, a bit of a slog. Learning quantum gates is like learning basic vocabulary in a new language: there’s no getting round spending a fair bit of time working on it. Still, the spaced-repetition testing should make this basic memory work much easier than is ordinarily the case. And it will prepare you well for some of the more conceptual issues discussed in Part III, where we return to high-level questions about the ultimate nature of computation, and what quantum computers are good for.</p><h2 id="quantum-not-gate">The quantum NOT gate</h2><p>Let’s take a look at our very first quantum logic gate, the <em>quantum NOT gate</em>. As you can no doubt surmise, the quantum NOT gate is a generalization of the classical NOT gate. On the computational basis states the quantum NOT gate does just what you’d expect, mimicking the classical NOT gate. That is, it takes the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> state to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> and vice versa:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mi>O</mi><mi>T</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">NOT|0\rangle = |1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">N</span><span class="mord mathdefault">O</span><span class="mord mathdefault">T</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mi>O</mi><mi>T</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">NOT|1\rangle = |0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">N</span><span class="mord mathdefault">O</span><span class="mord mathdefault">T</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>But the computational basis states aren’t the only states possible for a qubit. What happens when we apply the NOT gate to a general superposition state, that is, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>?</span> In fact, it does pretty much the simplest possible thing: it acts linearly on the quantum state, interchanging the role of <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mi>O</mi><mi>T</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">.</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>I’ve been using the notation NOT for the quantum NOT gate. But for historical reasons people working on quantum computing usually use a different notation, the notation <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>s</mi><mi>x</mi></msub></mrow><annotation class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> (often written <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>σ</mi><mi>x</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\sigma_x</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathdefault">σ</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathdefault mtight">x</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> in textbooks today) to help describe rotations of certain objects around the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span></span></span></span></span> spatial axis. This operation later became of interest to people working on quantum computing. But by that point the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">s</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">s</span></span></span></span></span> (and the connection to rotation) was irrelevant, and so <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>s</mi><mi>x</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">s_x</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathdefault">s</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathdefault mtight">x</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> just became <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span>.</span></p><p>What we’ve seen so far are very algebraic ways of writing down the way the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gate works. There’s an alternate representation, the <em>quantum circuit</em> representation. In a quantum circuit we depict an <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gate as follows:</p><p></p><center><img src="/assets/X_gate.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/X_gate-1x.png, /assets/X_gate.png 2x"></center><p></p><p>The line from left to right is what’s called a <em>quantum wire</em>. A quantum wire represents a single qubit. The term “wire” and the way it’s drawn looks like the qubit is moving through space. But it's often helpful to instead think of left-to-right as representing the passage of time. So the initial segment of wire is just the passage of time, with nothing happening to the qubit. Then the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gate is applied. And then the quantum wire continues, leaving the desired output.</p><p>Sometimes we’ll put the input and output states explicitly in the quantum circuit, so we have something like:</p><p></p><center><img src="/assets/X_gate_decorated.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/X_gate_decorated-1x.png, /assets/X_gate_decorated.png 2x"></center><p></p><p>So that’s the quantum circuit representation of the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gate. It is, in fact, our first quantum computation. A simple computation, involving just a single qubit and a single gate, but a genuine quantum computation nonetheless!</p><p>There’s a third representation for the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gate that’s worth knowing about, a representation as a <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2 \times 2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" In fact, as we’ll see later, it turns out that all quantum gates can be thought of as matrices, with the matrix entries specifying the exact details of the gate.</p><p>By the way, regarding the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gate as a matrix clarifies what may have been a confusing point earlier. stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><h2 id="quantum-wires">Quantum wires: why the simplest quantum circuit is often also the hardest to implement</h2><p>We’ve now seen a simple quantum circuit and quantum logic gate. But it’s not quite the simplest possible quantum circuit. The simplest possible quantum circuit does nothing at all. That is, it’s a single quantum wire:</p><p></p><center><img src="/assets/identity_circuit.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/identity_circuit-1x.png, /assets/identity_circuit.png 2x"></center><p></p><p>This circuit is just a single qubit being preserved in time. To be more explicit, if some arbitrary quantum state <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> is input to the circuit, then the exact same state <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> is output (it’s common practice to use the Greek letter <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>ψ</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\psi</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span></span></span></span></span> to denote an arbitrary quantum state):</p><p></p><center><img src="/assets/identity_circuit_labelled.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/identity_circuit_labelled-1x.png, /assets/identity_circuit_labelled.png 2x"></center><p></p><p>Mathematically, this circuit is trivial. But physically it’s far from trivial. In many physical systems, the quantum wire is actually one of the hardest quantum computations to implement!</p><p>The reason is that quantum states are often incredibly fragile. If your qubit is being stored in some tiny system – perhaps a single photon or a single atom – then it’s very, very easy to disturb that state. It really doesn’t take much to upset an atom or a photon. And so while quantum wires are mathematically trivial, they can be one of the hardest elements to build in real systems.</p><p>That said, there are some systems where quantum wires are easy to implement. If you store your qubit in a neutrino then the state will actually be pretty well preserved. The reason is that neutrinos barely interact with other forms of matter at all – a neutrino can easily pass through a mile of lead without being disturbed. But while it’s intriguing that neutrinos are so stable, it doesn’t mean they make good qubits. The trouble is that since there’s no easy way of using ordinary matter to interact with them, we can’t manipulate their quantum state in a controlled fashion, and so can’t implement a quantum gate.</p><p>There’s a tension here that applies to many proposals to do quantum information processing, not just neutrinos. If we want to store the quantum state, then it’s helpful if our qubits only interact very weakly with other systems, so those systems don’t disrupt them. But if the qubits only interact weakly with other systems then that also makes it hard to manipulate the qubits. Thus, systems which make good quantum wires are often hard to build quantum gates for. Much of the art of designing quantum computers is about finding ways to navigate this tension. Often, that means trying to design systems which interact weakly most of the time, but some of the time can be caused to interact strongly, and so serve as part of a quantum gate.</p><div class="review_set" id="reviewSet-5"><div class="ReviewArea" touch-action="manipulation"><div class="BottomControlArea"><div class="ReviewButtonArea"><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 31 31" fill="none"><path d="M15.5 7C10.2533 7 6 11.2533 6 16.5C6 21.7467 10.2533 26 15.5 26C20.7467 26 25 21.7467 25 16.5C25 15.3037 24.7789 14.159 24.3752 13.1046" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round"></path><path d="M15 11.5V2.5L22 7L15 11.5Z"></path></svg>Didn’t remember</button><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 33 33" fill="none"><path d="M5.99981 18.5564L13.778 26.3345L26.7416 8.18545" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><p>You’ll note that some of the questions above have a different flavor to the questions earlier in the essay. Early questions had cut-and-dried answers. The answer to the question “What’s the standard notation for the quantum NOT gate?” is just: “<span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span>”</span>. But some of the questions above have less well-specified answers. They’re a little fluffy.</p><p>This fluffiness may cause you difficulties as you decide how to respond. Your answer to the question “Why is it that systems which make good quantum wires are often hard to build quantum gates for?” may not quite match the answer given. If this is the case, don’t worry. You should mark yourself correct if you’re confident you’ve understood the point, even in terms different from my phrasing. And mark yourself incorrect if the point still needs reinforcement.</p><h2 id="multi-gate-quantum-circuit">A multi-gate quantum circuit</h2><p>Let’s take a look at a simple multiple-gate quantum circuit. class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>That is, <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">XX</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> is the identity operation, and so the output <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi><mi>X</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">XX|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> is just the original input <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> In other words, two <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gates is the same as a quantum wire. And so we’ve arrived at the same conclusion in a different way, using matrix multiplication. Doing such matrix multiplications is a pretty common way of analyzing quantum circuits.</p><div class="review_set" id="reviewSet-6"><div class="ReviewArea" touch-action="manipulation"><div class="BottomControlArea"><div class="ReviewButtonArea"><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 31 31" fill="none"><path d="M15.5 7C10.2533 7 6 11.2533 6 16.5C6 21.7467 10.2533 26 15.5 26C20.7467 26 25 21.7467 25 16.5C25 15.3037 24.7789 14.159 24.3752 13.1046" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round"></path><path d="M15 11.5V2.5L22 7L15 11.5Z"></path></svg>Didn’t remember</button><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 33 33" fill="none"><path d="M5.99981 18.5564L13.778 26.3345L26.7416 8.18545" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><h2 id="the-hadamard-gate">The Hadamard gate</h2><p>We’ve seen our first quantum gate, the NOT or <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gate. Of course, the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> didn’t appear to do all that much beyond what is possible with a classical NOT gate. In this section I introduce a gate that clearly involves quantum effects, the <em>Hadamard</em> gate.</p><p>As with the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gate, we’ll start by explaining how the Hadamard gate acts on computational basis states. Denoting the gate by <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> here’s what it does:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mfrac></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H|0\rangle = \frac{|0\rangle +|1\rangle}{\sqrt 2}</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mopen nulldelimiter"></span><span class="mfrac"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord sqrt"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class="svg-align"><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord">2</span></span><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="hide-tail"><svg viewbox="0 0 400000 1080" preserveaspectratio="xMinYMin slice"><path d="M95,702c-2.7,0,-7.17,-2.7,-13.5,-8c-5.8,-5.3,-9.5,

How does the Hadamard gate act on more general quantum states?</p><p>It won’t surprise you to learn that it acts linearly, as did the quantum NOT gate. In particular, the Hadamard gate takes a superposition <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha|0\rangle + \beta|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> to the corresponding superposition of outputs:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>α</mi><mrow><mo fence="true">(</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mfrac><mo fence="true">)</mo></mrow><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mrow><mo fence="true">(</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>−</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mfrac><mo fence="true">)</mo></mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">.</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H(\alpha|0\rangle + \beta|1\rangle) = \alpha \left( \frac{|0\rangle+|1\rangle}{\sqrt 2} \right) We can make it less of a mess by combining the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> terms together, and also the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> terms together:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi></mrow><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mfrac><mi \frac{\alpha+\beta}{\sqrt 2} |0\rangle + \frac{\alpha-\beta}{\sqrt 2}|1\rangle.</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span Fortunately, we mostly won’t be dealing with such complex expressions. The only reason I’ve done it here is to be really explicit. Instead of dealing with such explicit expressions, we’ll mostly work with the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span> gate in its circuit and matrix representations (see below). Those let us focus at a more enlightening level of abstraction, rather than messing around with coefficients.</p><p>Indeed, much work on quantum computing is about attempting to develop ways of moving from low levels of abstraction to higher, more conceptual levels. Up to now most of our work has been at a very low level, seeming more an exercise in linear algebra than a discussion of a new model of computing. That perhaps seems strange. After all, if you were explaining classical computers to someone, you wouldn’t start in the weeds, with AND and NOT gates and the like. You’d start with a well-designed high-level programming language, and then bounce back and forth between different layers of abstraction. Modern computers aren’t just about logic gates – they’re at least as much about beautiful higher-level ideas: say, lazy evaluation, or higher-order functions, or homoiconicity, and so on.</p><p>I wish I could start with high-level abstractions for quantum computers. However, we’re still in the early days of quantum computing, and for the most part humanity hasn’t yet discovered such high-level abstractions. People are still scratching around, trying to find good ideas.</p><p>That’s an exciting situation: it means almost all the big breakthroughs are ahead. There’s a sense in which we still understand very little about quantum computing. That might sound surprising: after all, there are <a href="https://www.amazon.com/Quantum-Computation-Information-10th-Anniversary/dp/1107002176/ref=sr_1_2?s=books&amp;ie=UTF8&amp;qid=1541350337&amp;sr=1-2">great big fat textbooks on the subject</a>. But you could have written a great big fat textbook about the ENIAC computer in the late 1940s. It was, after all, a very complex system. That textbook would have looked intimidating, but it wouldn’t have been the final word in computing. For the most part the way we understand quantum computing today is at an ENIAC-like level, looking at the nuts-and-bolts of qubits and logic gates and linear algebra, and wondering what the higher-level understanding may be. The situation can be thought of as much like programming language design before the breakthroughs that led to languages such as Lisp and Haskell and Prolog and Smalltalk. That makes it a remarkable creative opportunity, a challenge for the decades and centuries ahead.</p><p>Speaking of nuts-and-bolts, let’s get back to the Hadamard gate. Here’s the circuit representation for the Hadamard gate. It looks just like the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> gate in the circuit representation, except we change the gate label to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span>:</span></p><p></p><center><img src="/assets/H_gate.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/H_gate-1x.png, /assets/H_gate.png 2x"></center><p></p><p>Just like the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation class="mord"><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">−</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>So the matrix acts the same way as the Hadamard gate on both the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> states. By the linearity of matrix multiplication it follows that the matrix acts the same way as the Hadamard on all input states, and so they are the same operation.</p><p>What makes the Hadamard gate interesting as a quantum gate? What can we use it to do?</p><p>We don’t (quite) have enough background to give precise answers to these questions yet. But there is an analogy which gives insight.</p><p>Imagine you are living in North Africa, thousands of years in the past, and decide for some reason that you want to get over to the Iberian peninsula. If you don’t yet have boats or some other reliable method of moving across large bodies of water, you need to go all the way across Africa, past the Arabian peninsula, up and around through Europe, back to the Iberian peninsula:</p><img src="/assets/Africa_Europe_by_land.jpg" class="FullBleed"><p class="ImageCaption">Original photo source: Reto Stöckli, <a href="https://visibleearth.nasa.gov/view.php?id=73884">NASA Earth Observatory</a> (2004).</p><p>Suppose, however, that you invent a new device, the boat, which expands the range of locations you can traverse. Then you can take a much more direct route over to the Iberian peninsula, greatly cutting down the time required:</p><img src="/assets/Africa_Europe_by_sea.jpg" class="FullBleed"><p>What the Hadamard and similar gates do is expand the range of operations that it’s possible for a computer to perform. That expansion makes it possible for the computer to take shortcuts, as the computer “moves” in a way that’s not possible in a conventional classical computer. And, we hope, that may enable us to solve some computational problems faster.</p><p>Another helpful analogy is to the game of chess. Imagine you’re playing chess and the rules are changed in your favor, enabling your rook an expanded range of moves. That extra flexibility might enable you to achieve checkmate much faster because you can get to new positions much more quickly.</p><p>A similar thing is going on with the Hadamard gate. By expanding the range of states we can access (or, more precisely, the range of dynamical operations we can generate) beyond what’s possible on a classical computer, it becomes possible to take shortcuts in our computation.</p><p>We’ll see explicit examples in subsequent essays.</p><p id="hadamard-twice">To get more familiar with the Hadamard gate, let’s analyze a simple circuit:</p><p></p><center><img src="/assets/H_H_circuit.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/H_H_circuit-1x.png, /assets/H_H_circuit.png 2x"></center><p></p><p>What’s this circuit do?</p><p>Before we compute, it’s worth pausing for a second to try guessing the result. The point of guessing isn’t to get it right – rather, it’s to challenge yourself to start coming up with heuristic mental models for thinking about what’s going on in quantum circuits. Those mental models likely won’t be very good at first, but that’s okay – if you keep doing this, they’ll get better.</p><p>Here’s one heuristic you can use to think about this circuit: you can think of <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span> as sort of mixing the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> states together. So if you apply <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span> twice to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> perhaps it would thoroughly mix the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> up together. What sort of state do you think would result, according to this heuristic? Do you believe the result? Why or why not? Can you think of other heuristics that might help you guess an answer?</p><p>Alright, let’s compute what actually happens. After we apply the first Hadamard to <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> we get</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mfrac><mi mathvariant="normal">.</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\frac{|0\rangle+|1\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}.</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mopen nulldelimiter"></span><span class="mfrac"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord sqrt"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class="svg-align"><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">2</span></span></span><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="hide-tail"><svg viewbox="0 0 400000 1080" preserveaspectratio="xMinYMin slice"><path d="M95,702c-2.7,0,-7.17,-2.7,-13.5,-8c-5.8,-5.3,-9.5,

When we collect up these terms, we see that the output is just the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> state, same as we started with. And so both the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> states are left unchanged by this quantum circuit, and the net effect of the circuit is exactly the same as a quantum wire:</p><p><img src="/assets/H_H_identity.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/H_H_identity-1x.png, /assets/H_H_identity.png 2x"></p><p>There’s an alternate way of seeing this, which I’ll sketch out without working through in detail. It’s to note that if we input an arbitrary quantum state <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> to the circuit, then the output must be <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mi>H</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H H |\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> i.e., the result of applying two <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span> matrices to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> But if you just compute the matrix product <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H H</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span> it turns out to be the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2 \times 2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">2</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">×</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">2</span></span></span></span></span> identity matrix, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mi>H</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>I</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H H = I</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">I</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> And so the output from the circuit must be the same as the input, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mi>H</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>I</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">HH|\psi\rangle = I |\psi\rangle = |\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">I</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> just as from a quantum wire.</p><p>Of course, this result violates our intuitive guess, which was that two Hadamards would thoroughly mix up the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and the <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> It’s interesting to ponder what went wrong with our intuition, say by looking through the calculation for <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span> acting twice on <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> You see that after the second gate the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> terms exactly cancel one another out, while the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> terms reinforce one another.</p><p>This seems innocuous, almost like a mathematical accident. Still, I draw your attention to it because this type of cancellation or reinforcement is crucial in many algorithms for quantum computers. Without getting into details, the rough way many such algorithms work is to first use Hadamard gates to “spread out” in quantum states like <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mfrac></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\frac{|0\rangle+|1\rangle}{\sqrt 2}</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mopen nulldelimiter"></span><span class="mfrac"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord sqrt mtight"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class="svg-align"><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord mtight">2</span></span><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="hide-tail mtight"><svg viewbox="0 0 400000 1080" preserveaspectratio="xMinYMin slice"><path d="M95,702c-2.7,0,-7.17,-2.7,-13.5,-8c-5.8,-5.3,-9.5,

s-235,486,-235,486c-2.7,4.7,-9,7,-19,7c-6,0,-10,-1,-12,-3s-194,-422,-194,-422
s-65,47,-65,47z M834 80H400000v40H845z"></path></svg></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="frac-line"></span></span><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord mtight">∣</span><span class="mord mtight">0</span><span class="mclose mtight">⟩</span><span class="mbin mtight">+</span><span class="mord mtight">∣</span><span class="mord mtight">1</span><span class="mclose mtight">⟩</span></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mclose nulldelimiter"></span></span></span></span></span></span> (or many-qubit analogs), i.e., in superpositions of multiple computational basis states. At the end of the algorithm they use clever patterns of cancellation and reinforcement to bring things back together again into one (or possibly a few, in the many-qubit case) computational basis state, containing the desired answer. That’s a somewhat vague and perhaps tantalizing description, but the point to take away is that the kind of cancellation-or-reinforcement we saw above is actually crucial in many quantum computations.</p><div class="exercises"><p>I’ll now pose a few simple exercises related to the Hadamard gate. Unlike the spaced-repetition questions, the point of the exercises below isn’t as an aid to memory, and so you won’t see these exercises repeatedly. Rather, they’re here because (should you choose to work through them) they will help you better understand the material of the essay. But they’ll only incidentally help with memorization. We’ll follow them with some spaced-repetition questions. Note that even if you don't work through the exercises, it's worth at least reading through them, since some of the results will be tested in the spaced-repetition questions.</p><p><strong>Exercise:</strong> Verify that <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mi>H</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>I</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">HH = I</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">I</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> where <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">I</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">I</span></span></span></span></span> is the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2 \times 2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">2</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">×</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">2</span></span></span></span></span> identity matrix, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>I</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo fence="true">[</mo><mtable columnalign="center center" columnspacing="1em" <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>J</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">J</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" gate?</p><p><strong>Exercise:</strong> Consider the quantum circuit:</p><img src="/assets/H_X_circuit.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/H_X_circuit-1x.png, /assets/H_X_circuit.png 2x"><p>Explain why the output from this circuit is <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi><mi>H</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">XH|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> not <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mi>X</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">HX|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> as you might naively assume if you wrote down gates in the order they occur in the circuit. This is a common gotcha to be aware of – it occurs because quantum gates compose left-to-right in the circuit representation, while matrix multiplications compose right-to-left.</p></div><div class="review_set" id="reviewSet-7"><div class="ReviewArea" touch-action="manipulation"><div class="BottomControlArea"><div class="ReviewButtonArea"><button class="Button primary regular Disabled"
