stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><h2 id="measuring_a_qubit">Measuring a qubit</h2><p>Suppose a (hypothetical!) quantum physicist named Alice prepares a qubit in her laboratory, in a quantum state <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> Then she gives her qubit to another quantum physicist, Bob, but doesn’t tell him the values of <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>β</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\beta</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> Is there some way Bob can figure out <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>β</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\beta</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span></span></span></span></span>?</span> That is, is there some experiment Bob can do to figure out the identity of the quantum state?</p><p>The surprising answer to this question turns out to be <em>NO!</em> There is, in fact, no way to figure out <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>β</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\beta</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span></span></span></span></span> if they start out unknown. To put it a slightly different way, the quantum state of any system – whether it be a qubit or a some other system – is not directly observable.</p><p>I say this is surprising, because it’s very different from our usual everyday way of thinking about how the world works. If there’s something wrong with your car, a mechanic can use diagnostic tools to learn about the internal state of the engine. The better the diagnostic tools, the more they can learn. Of course, there may be parts of the engine that would be impractical to access – maybe they’d have to break a part, or use a microscope, for instance. But you’d probably be rather suspicious if the mechanic told you the laws of physics prohibited them from figuring out the internal state of the engine.</p><p>Similarly, when you first start learning about quantum circuits, it seems like we should be able to observe the amplitudes of a quantum state whenever we like. But that turns out to be prohibited by the laws of physics. Those amplitudes are better thought of as a kind of hidden information.</p><p>So, what can we figure out from the quantum state? Rather than somehow measuring <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>β</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\beta</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> there are other ways of getting useful information out of a qubit. Let me describe an especially important process called <em>measurement in the computational basis</em>. This is a fundamental primitive in quantum computing: it’s the way we typically extract information from our quantum computers. I’ll explain now how it works for a single qubit, and later generalize to multi-qubit systems.</p><p>Suppose a qubit is in the state <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> When you measure this qubit in the computational basis it gives you a classical bit of information: it gives you the outcome <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span> with probability <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>α</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\alpha|^2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>,</span> and the outcome <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span> with probability <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>β</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\beta|^2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</span></p><p>To think a little more concretely about this process, suppose your qubit is instantiated in some physical system. Perhaps it’s being stored in the state of an atom somehow. It doesn’t matter exactly what, but you have this qubit in your laboratory. And you have some measurement apparatus, probably something large and complicated, maybe involving lasers and microprocessors and a screen for readout of the measurement result. And this measurement apparatus interacts in some way with your qubit.</p><p>After the measurement interaction, your measurement apparatus registers an outcome. For instance, it might be that you get the outcome <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> Or maybe instead you get the outcome <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> The crucial fact is that the outcome is ordinary classical information – the stuff you already know how to think about – which you can then use to do other things, and to control other processes.</p><p>So the way a quantum computation works is that we manipulate a quantum state using a series of quantum gates, and then at the end of the computation (typically) we do a measurement to read out the result of the computation. If our quantum computer is just a single qubit, then that result will be a single classical bit. If, as is more usually the case, it’s multiple qubits, then the measurement result will be multiple classical bits.</p><p>A fundamental fact about this measurement process is that it disturbs the state of the quantum system. In particular, it doesn’t just leave the quantum state alone. After the measurement, if you get the outcome <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span> then the state of the qubit afterwards (the “posterior state”) is the computational basis state <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> On the other hand, if you get the outcome <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span> then the posterior state of the qubit is the computational basis state <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span></p><p>Summing all this up: if we measure a qubit with state <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha |0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> in the computational basis, then the outcome is a classical bit: either <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> with probability <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>α</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\alpha|^2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>,</span> or <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> with probability <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>β</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\beta|^2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</span> The corresponding state of the qubit after the measurement is <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> or <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span></p><p>A key point to note is that after the measurement, no matter what the outcome, <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>β</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\beta</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span></span></span></span></span> are gone. No matter whether the posterior state is <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> or <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> there is no trace of <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> or <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>β</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\beta</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> And so you can’t get any more information about them. In that sense, <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>β</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\beta</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span></span></span></span></span> are a kind of hidden information – the measurement doesn’t tell you what they were.</p><p>One reason this is important is because it means you can’t store an infinite amount of classical information in a qubit. After all, <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> is a complex number, and you could imagine storing lots of classical bits in the binary expansion of the real component of <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> If there was some experimental way you could measure the value of <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> exactly, then you could extract that classical information. But without a way of measuring <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span></span></span></span></span> that’s not possible.</p><p>I’ve been talking about measurement in the computational basis. In fact, there are other types of measurement you can do in quantum systems. But there’s a sense in which computational basis measurements turn out to be fundamental. The reason is that by combining computational basis measurements with quantum gates like the Hadamard and NOT (and other) gates, it’s possible to simulate arbitrary quantum measurements. So this is all you absolutely need to know about measurement, from an in-principle point of view.</p><p>It’s useful to have a way of denoting measurements in the quantum circuit model. Here’s a simple example:</p><p><img src="/assets/X_H_measure.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/X_H_measure-1x.png, /assets/X_H_measure.png 2x"></p><p>It’s a single-qubit quantum circuit, with input the state <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> A NOT gate is applied, followed by a Hadamard gate. The circuit finishes with a measurement in the computational basis, denoted by the slightly elongated semi-circle. The <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">m</span></span></span></span></span> is a classical bit denoting the measurement result – either <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span> or <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span> – and we use the double wire to indicate the classical bit <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">m</span></span></span></span></span> going off and being used to do something else.</p><p>Of course, I said above that after measurement the qubit is in either the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> or the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> state. You might think we’d draw a corresponding quantum wire coming out the other side of the measurement. But often in quantum circuits the qubit is discarded after measurement, and that’s assumed by this notation.</p><p>One final comment on measurement is that it’s connected to the normalization condition for quantum states that we discussed earlier. Suppose we have the quantum state:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>Then the probability of the two possible measurement outcomes, <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> must sum to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> and so we have:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>α</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>β</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>1.</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\alpha|^2+|\beta|^2 = 1.</annotation></semantics></math></span><span id="general-single-qubit-gates">General single-qubit gates</h2><p>So far we’ve learned about two quantum gates, the NOT and the Hadamard gate, and also about the measurement process that can be used to extract classical information from our quantum circuits. In this section, I return to quantum gates, and take a look at the most general single-qubit gate. To do that it helps to recall the matrix representations of the NOT and Hadamard gates:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mtable class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> then the output is <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>H</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">H|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> respectively.</p><p>A general single-qubit gate works similarly. In particular, a general single-qubit gate can be represented as a <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2 \times 2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">2</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">×</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">2</span></span></span></span></span> unitary matrix, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> (If you’re rusty, I’ll remind you what it means for a matrix to be unitary in a moment, and you can just think of it as a matrix for now.) If the input to the gate is the state <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> then the output from the gate is <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> And so the NOT and Hadamard gates correspond to the special cases where <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U = X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>H</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U = H</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> respectively.</p><p>What does it mean for a matrix <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span></span></span></span></span> to be unitary? It’s easiest to answer this question algebraically, where it simply means that <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>U</mi><mo>†</mo></msup><mi>U</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>I</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U^\dagger U = I</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mbin mtight">†</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">I</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> that is, the adjoint of <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> denoted <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>U</mi><mo>†</mo></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U^\dagger</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mbin mtight">†</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>,</span> times <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> is equal to the identity matrix. That adjoint is, recall, the complex transpose of <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span></span></span></span></span>:</span></p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>U</mi><mo>†</mo></msup><mo>:</mo><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>U</mi><mi>T</mi></msup><msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>∗</mo></msup><mi mathvariant="normal">.</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U^\dagger := (U^T)^*.</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mbin mtight">†</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">:</span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathdefault mtight">T</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mclose"><span class="mclose">)</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mbin mtight">∗</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mord">.</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>So for a <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2 \times 2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">2</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">×</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">2</span></span></span></span></span> matrix, the adjoint operation is just:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msup><mrow><mo fence="true">[</mo><mtable columnalign="center center" columnspacing="1em" rowspacing="0.15999999999999992em"><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="false" scriptlevel="0"><mi>a</mi></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="false" scriptlevel="0"><msup><mi>c</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup></mstyle></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="false" scriptlevel="0"><msup><mi>b</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle mtight"><span class="mbin mtight">∗</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord">.</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>(Note that the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo>†</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\dagger</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">†</span></span></span></span></span> is also sometimes called the <em>dagger operation</em>, or <em>Hermitian conjugation</em>, or just the <em>conjugation</em> operation. We’ll use all three terms on occasion.)</p><p>There are a few basic questions you might ask: why are single-qubit gates described by unitary matrices? And how can we get an intuitive feel for what it means for a matrix to be unitary, anyway? While the equation <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>U</mi><mo>†</mo></msup><mi>U</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>I</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U^\dagger U = I</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mbin mtight">†</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">I</span></span></span></span></span> is easy to check algebraically, we’d like some intuition for what that equation means.</p><p>Another natural question is whether the NOT gate and the Hadamard gate are unitary? Of course, we’ll see that they are – I wouldn’t have described them as quantum gates if not – but we should go to the trouble of checking.</p><p>Yet another good question is whether there are useful examples of single-qubit gates that aren’t the NOT or Hadamard gates? The equation <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>U</mi><mo>†</mo></msup><mi>U</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>I</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U^\dagger U = I</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mbin mtight">†</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">I</span></span></span></span></span> is all very well, but it’d be nice to have more concrete examples than just an abstract equation.</p><p>We’ll answer all these questions over the next few sections.</p><p>Let’s start by checking the unitarity of the Hadamard gate. mathdefault">H</span></span></span></span></span>?</span></p></div><p>For flavor, let’s give a few more examples of single-qubit quantum gates. Earlier in the essay, I mentioned that the NOT gate <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span></span></span></span></span> was introduced by the physicist Wolfgang Pauli in the early days of quantum mechanics. He introduced two other matrices, <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>Y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Y</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">Y</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>Z</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Z</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">Z</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> which are also useful quantum gates. The three gates, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>Y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">X, Y</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">X</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">Y</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>Z</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Z</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">Z</span></span></span></span></span> are known collectively as the <em>Pauli matrices</em>. The <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>Y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Y</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">Y</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>Z</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Z</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">Z</span></span></span></span></span> gates will be useful extra tools in our toolkit of quantum gates; in terms of the earlier analogy they expand the repertoire of moves we have available to us. They’re crucial, for example, in protocols such as quantum teleportation and quantum error correction.</p><p>The <span><span class="katex"><span mathdefault">Z</span></span></span></span></span> matrices are unitary, and so legitimate quantum gates.</p></div><p>Another good example of a quantum gate is a rotation, the kind of matrix you’ve class="mord">.</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>You can easily check that this is a unitary matrix, and so it’s valid quantum gate. And sometimes it’s a very useful quantum gate!</p><p>That mostly wraps up our little library of single-qubit gates. In the next couple of sections we’ll build up some intuition about what unitarity means, but we won’t add any extra elements to our quantum computing model. In fact, at this point we know almost everything needed for quantum computation. There are just two extra elements needed: extending our description of single qubits to multiple qubits, and describing a simple two-qubit quantum gate. We’ll get to those things shortly – not surprisingly, they look much like what we’ve already seen.</p><div class="review_set" id="reviewSet-9"><div class="ReviewArea" touch-action="manipulation"><div class="BottomControlArea"><div class="ReviewButtonArea"><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 31 31" fill="none"><path d="M15.5 7C10.2533 7 6 11.2533 6 16.5C6 21.7467 10.2533 26 15.5 26C20.7467 26 25 21.7467 25 16.5C25 15.3037 24.7789 14.159 24.3752 13.1046" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round"></path><path d="M15 11.5V2.5L22 7L15 11.5Z"></path></svg>Didn’t remember</button><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 33 33" fill="none"><path d="M5.99981 18.5564L13.778 26.3345L26.7416 8.18545" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><p>I am, by the way, somewhat uncomfortable with some of the questions just asked. My personal experience is that spaced-repetition learning works well when learning facts I have a lot of context for, and care a lot about. It’s a rare person who finds the detailed entries in a unitary matrix fascinating! That’s not to say they aren’t – actually, they <em>are</em>, but you need a lot more context than I’ve provided to see why (for instance) that <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo>−</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">-i</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">−</span><span class="mord mathdefault">i</span></span></span></span></span> in the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>Y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Y</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">Y</span></span></span></span></span> is just so darn interesting.</p><p>With that said, this essay is genuinely an experiment, and the questions above are included in that spirit. Maybe it will turn out that readers can use spaced-repetition to learn the entries of unitary matrices. And maybe they cannot. No matter which that’ll be a valuable thing to learn about the world, and to inform future experiments with spaced-repetition learning.</p><h2 id="what-does-it-mean-to-be-unitary">What does it mean for a matrix to be unitary?</h2><p>Can we get an intuition for what it means for a matrix to be unitary? It turns out that unitary matrices <em>preserve the length of their inputs</em>. In other words, if we take any vector <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and compute the length <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi>U</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\| U|\psi\rangle \|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∥</span></span></span></span></span> it’s always equal to the length <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\||\psi\rangle\|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∥</span></span></span></span></span> of the original vector. In this, they’re much like rotations or reflections in ordinary (real) space, which also don’t change lengths. In a sense, the unitary matrices are a complex generalization of real rotations and reflections.</p><p>Up to now we’ve been dealing mostly with vectors which are states of qubits, i.e., normalized 2-dimensional vectors. But in fact the statement of the last paragraph is true for <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">d \times d</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">d</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">×</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">d</span></span></span></span></span> unitary matrices, too, i.e., <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi>U</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\|U|\psi\rangle\| = \||\psi\rangle\|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∥</span></span></span></span></span> is true for any vector <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> in <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>d</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">d</span></span></span></span></span> dimensions.</p><p>The proof is actually pretty simple – mostly, we just compute <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi>U</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\|U|\psi\rangle \|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∥</span></span></span></span></span> and use a little algebra to check that it’s equal to the length <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\||\psi\rangle\|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∥</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> But before we get to the proof, observe that this result is good news for quantum gates. The reason is that in a quantum gate the input state will be normalized (have length <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>)</span>, since that’s a requirement for a quantum state. And we’d expect the corresponding output state to also be normalized, otherwise it wouldn’t be a legitimate quantum state. Fortunately, the length-preserving property of unitary matrices ensures the output state is properly normalized.</p><p>Recall also the larger story this is part of: not only are gates unitary, to ensure that states remain normalized; also, quantum states are normalized, since measurement probabilities must sum to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> It all fits together.</p><p>In fact it turns out that unitary matrices are the <em>only</em> matrices which preserve length in this way. And so a good way of thinking about unitary matrices is that they’re exactly the class of matrices which preserve length. That’s the geometric interpretation (and intuitive meaning) of the algebraic condition <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>U</mi><mo>†</mo></msup><mi>U</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>I</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U^\dagger U = I</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mbin mtight">†</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">I</span></span></span></span></span>.</span></p><p>Let’s prove now that unitary matrices really are length-preserving.</p><p>The proof is straightforward. The main thing is to compute <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi>U</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\|U|\psi\rangle\|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∥</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> Actually, it’s a little easier to compute the square of the length, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi>U</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\|U|\psi\rangle\|^2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</span> This is just the sum of the squares of the absolute values of the components of the vector <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>:</span></p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi>U</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>=</mo><munder><mo>∑</mo><mi>j</mi></munder><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>U</mi><mi>ψ</mi><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>j</mi><mo>∗</mo></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>U</mi><mi>ψ</mi><msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow><annotation size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span mentioned earlier, it’s also possible to prove that unitary matrices are the only matrices which preserve lengths in this way. Let me state that a little more precisely:</p><p><strong>Theorem:</strong> Let <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">M</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">M</span></span></span></span></span> be a matrix. Then <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi>M</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\|M|\psi\rangle \| = \||\psi\rangle\|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord mathdefault">M</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∥</span></span></span></span></span> for all vectors <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> if and only if <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">M</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">M</span></span></span></span></span> is unitary.</p><p>We’ve proved one half of this already; we’ll prove the other half in the next section. This theorem answers many of our questions from earlier: we see why quantum gates must be unitary, since they’re the only matrices which preserve normalization. Of course, it doesn’t completely answer the question, since it doesn’t tell us why quantum gates should be matrices (i.e., linear operations) in the first place. That fact we’re simply going to accept. In fact, it is possible to develop deeper levels of understanding of why that is true, but in this essay we’ll be satisfied with the partial explanation that unitary matrices are length-preserving.</p><div class="review_set" id="reviewSet-10"><div class="ReviewArea" touch-action="manipulation"><div class="BottomControlArea"><div class="ReviewButtonArea"><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 31 31" fill="none"><path d="M15.5 7C10.2533 7 6 11.2533 6 16.5C6 21.7467 10.2533 26 15.5 26C20.7467 26 25 21.7467 25 16.5C25 15.3037 24.7789 14.159 24.3752 13.1046" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round"></path><path d="M15 11.5V2.5L22 7L15 11.5Z"></path></svg>Didn’t remember</button><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 33 33" fill="none"><path d="M5.99981 18.5564L13.778 26.3345L26.7416 8.18545" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><h2 id="why-unitaries-only-matrices-preserve-length">Why are unitaries the only matrices which preserve length?</h2><p>Alright, let’s prove the missing part from the last section: let’s show that unitaries are the only matrices which preserve length.</p><p>The proof is a little messy. But it turns out to be a good way to get familiar with a few extra pieces of standard quantum mechanical terminology and notation. I’ll be frank: while these pieces of terminology are extremely useful in quantum computing, we don’t strictly need them elsewhere in this essay (though we will in later essays). If you want to skip the section, or skim it, that’s okay – this is the best section of the essay to skip. But at some point you should come back and work through the material. And there is, in any case, a certain beauty to the proof.</p><p>We’ve been dealing with 2-dimensional vectors up to now, but what I’m about to say applies no matter how many dimensions we’re working in. So suppose we have a vector <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span encoding="application/x-tex">\langle\psi|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mopen">⟨</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mord">∣</span></span></span></span></span> is a row vector, whose entries are the same as <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> but complex conjugated. The vector <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> was called a ket, and (you’re going to groan) <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">⟨</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\langle \psi|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mopen">⟨</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mord">∣</span></span></span></span></span> is called a <em>bra</em>, making this the <em>bra-ket</em>, or <em>bracket</em> notation. Yes, theoretical physicists make dad jokes, too. These names were given by the theoretical physicist Paul Dirac in 1939, and it’s often called the Dirac bra-ket notation, or sometimes just the Dirac notation.</p><p>A key fact about the bra <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">⟨</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\langle \psi|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mopen">⟨</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mord">∣</span></span></span></span></span> is that it’s related to the ket <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> by the dagger operation:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><msup><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>†</mo></msup><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">⟨</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">.</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle^\dagger = \langle \psi|.</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose"><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mbin mtight">†</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mopen">⟨</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">.</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>It’s easy to see why this identity is true. Take the vector</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo fence="true">[</mo><mtable columnalign="center" columnspacing="1em" rowspacing="0.15999999999999992em"><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="false" class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mpunct">,</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>and apply the dagger operation, which means taking the transpose, turning it into a row vector with entries <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>b</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>z</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">a, b, \ldots, us <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">⟨</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi></mrow><annotation class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">⟨</mo><mi>ψ</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mo>†</mo></msup><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\langle \psi|^\dagger = class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span></p><p>Another useful identity expresses the length of <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> in terms of the Dirac notation:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">⟨</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">.</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>That is, the length squared is just equal to the product of the row vector <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">⟨</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\langle class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msup><mi>a</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup><msup><mi>b</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup><mo>…</mo><mo mathvariant="normal">⋮</mi><mpadded voffset="0em"><mspace mathbackground="black"></mspace></mpadded></mstyle></mtd></mtr></mtable><mo fence="true">]</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>a</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>b</mi><msup><mi class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">a</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span
