mtight"><span class="mord mtight">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="minner">…</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>And that, of course, is <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∥</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\||\psi\rangle\|^2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>,</span> just as we wanted.</p><p>Physicists using the Dirac notation don’t usually write <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">⟨</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\langle \psi| |\psi\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mopen">⟨</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> They simplify it slightly, omitting one of the vertical bars <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span></span></span></span></span> in the middle, and just write it as:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">⟨</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>ψ</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">.</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\langle \psi| \psi \rangle.</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mopen">⟨</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">ψ</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">.</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>It’s only a slight simplification, but this omission of the extra bar turns out to make life considerably It’s very convenient to be able to relate that property to familiar operations about matrix multiplication. That’s the essential source of the interest in the above identities.</p><p>Alright, we’ve been working through some heavy, detailed material. stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><h2 id="the-controlled-not-gate">The controlled-NOT gate</h2><p>We’ve developed most of the ideas needed to do universal quantum computing. We understand qubits, quantum states, and have a repertoire of quantum gates. However, all our gates involve just a single qubit. To compute, we need some way for qubits to interact with one another. That is, we need quantum gates which involve two (or more) qubits.</p><p>An example of such a gate is the controlled-NOT (or CNOT) gate. In the quantum circuit language we have two wires, representing two qubits, and the following notation to represent the CNOT gate:</p><p><img src="/assets/cnot.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/cnot-1x.png, /assets/cnot.png 2x"></p><p>The wire with the small, filled dot on it (the top wire, in this example) is called the <em>control</em> qubit, for reasons which will become clear in a moment. And the wire with the larger, unfilled circle on it is called the <em>target</em> qubit.</p><p>Up to now I haven’t said what the possible states of a two-qubit system are, but you can probably guess. We now have four computational basis states, corresponding to the four possible states of a two-bit system: <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>01</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|00\rangle, |01\rangle, |10\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>11</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|11\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> And, for a two-qubit system, not only can we have those four states, we can also have superpositions (i.e., linear combinations) of them:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>01</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>γ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>δ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>11</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha|00\rangle+\beta|01\rangle+\gamma |10\rangle+\delta|11\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">γ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">δ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>Here, the amplitudes <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>β</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>γ</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>δ</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \delta</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">γ</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">δ</span></span></span></span></span> are just complex numbers, and the sum of the squares of the absolute values is <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>α</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>β</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>γ</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mi class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> This is the same kind of normalization condition as we had for a single qubit.</p><p>Now, the CNOT gate is, much like the quantum NOT gate, inspired directly by a classical gate. What it does is very simple. If the control qubit is set to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> as in the states <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|10\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>11</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|11\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> then it flips (i.e., NOTs) the target qubit. And otherwise it does nothing. Writing out the action on all four computational basis states we have:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mtable columnalign="right left right" columnspacing="0em 1em" rowspacing="0.24999999999999992em"><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>If you’re familiar with classical programming languages, then you can think of the CNOT as a very simple kind of <code>if-then</code> statement: <code>if</code> the control qubit is set, <code>then</code> NOT the target qubit. But while simple, it can be used as a building block to build up other, more complex kinds of conditional behavior.</p><p>There’s a way of summing up all four of the equations above in a single equation. Suppose <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span></span></span></span></span> are classical bits, i.e., <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span> or <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> Then we can rewrite the equations above in a single equation as:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mtable columnalign="right left right" columnspacing="0em 1em" rowspacing="0.24999999999999992em"><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>x</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>→</mo></mrow></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>x</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>⊕</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">.</mi></mrow></mstyle></mtd></mtr></mtable><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\begin{aligned} |x, y\rangle &amp; \rightarrow &amp; |x, y\oplus x\rangle. \end{aligned}</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mtable"><span class="col-align-r"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span><span class="col-align-l"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord"></span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">→</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span><span class="arraycolsep"></span><span class="col-align-r"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">⊕</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">.</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>Note the commas inserted to make this easier to read – this is pretty common in working with multi-qubit states.</p><p>The above equation makes clear that the CNOT leaves the control qubit <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span></span></span></span></span> alone, but flips the target qubit <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span></span></span></span></span> if <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span></span></span></span></span> is set to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> Note that <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo>⊕</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\oplus</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">⊕</span></span></span></span></span> is addition modulo <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">2</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> where <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>⊕</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1 \oplus 1 = 0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">⊕</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> as we would expect from the fact that the CNOT takes <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>11</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|11\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|10\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span></p><p>That’s all there is to the CNOT. It’s really a very simple idea and quantum gate. Note that it of course acts linearly on superpositions of computational basis states, as we expect for a quantum gate. So:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mtable columnalign="right left" columnspacing="0em" rowspacing="0.24999999999999992em"><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow></mrow></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mpadded depth="0px"><mphantom><mo>→</mo></mphantom></mpadded><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>01</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>γ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>δ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>11</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow></mstyle></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow></mrow></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>→</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>01</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>γ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>11</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>δ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow></mstyle></mtd></mtr></mtable><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\begin{aligned} &amp; \hphantom{ \rightarrow } \alpha|00\rangle+\beta|01\rangle+\gamma |10\rangle+\delta|11\rangle \\ &amp; \rightarrow \alpha|00\rangle+\beta|01\rangle+\gamma |11\rangle+\delta|10\rangle \end{aligned}</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mtable"><span class="col-align-r"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"></span></span><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span><span class="col-align-l"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord"></span><span class="mord"><span class="vlist-t"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class=""><span class="mord"><span class="mrel">→</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">γ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">δ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord"></span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">→</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">γ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">δ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>And, though I won’t explicitly carry out the verification, the CNOT is unitary, and thus preserves the length of quantum states, as we expect.</p><p>Of course, the CNOT doesn’t just appear in two-qubit computations. It also appears in computations involving more qubits. Let’s suppose we have three qubits, for instance, and computational basis states such as <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>000</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>001</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|000\rangle, |001\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> and so on. Here’s a CNOT with the second qubit as the control qubit and the third qubit as the target:</p><p><img src="/assets/cnot23.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/cnot23-1x.png, /assets/cnot23.png 2x"></p><p>What goes on? Well, we can write out what happens on an arbitrary computational basis state, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>x</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>z</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|x, y, z\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">z</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> where <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x, y</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>z</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">z</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">z</span></span></span></span></span> are all classical bits. Of course, the first bit <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span></span></span></span></span> isn’t changed at all, since it’s not involved in the CNOT. The second bit <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span></span></span></span></span> is the control bit, and so isn’t changed. But the third bit <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>z</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">z</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">z</span></span></span></span></span> is flipped if the control bit <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span></span></span></span></span> is set to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> And so we can write the action of the CNOT as:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>x</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>z</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>→</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>x</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi>z</mi><mo>⊕</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|x,y,z\rangle \rightarrow |x,y, z\oplus y\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">z</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mrel">→</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">x</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">z</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">⊕</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">y</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>I’ve described the CNOT as a “classical” gate, but it can be combined with single-qubit gates to do non-classical things. Let me give you an explicit example. It’s another two-qubit computation. It starts with the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|00\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> computational basis state, we apply a Hadamard gate to the first qubit, and then do a CNOT:</p><p><img src="/assets/generate_entanglement.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/generate_entanglement-1x.png, /assets/generate_entanglement.png 2x"></p><p>Recall that for a single qubit the Hadamard gate takes <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> to an equal superposition <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">/</mi><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(|0\rangle+|1\rangle)/\sqrt{2}</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mclose">)</span><span class="mord">/</span><span class="mord sqrt"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class="svg-align"><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">2</span></span></span><span class=""><span class="pstrut"></span><span class="hide-tail"><svg viewbox="0 0 400000 1080" preserveaspectratio="xMinYMin slice"><path d="M95,702c-2.7,0,-7.17,-2.7,-13.5,-8c-5.8,-5.3,-9.5,

class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</span> For these two qubits it doesn’t affect the second qubit at all, and so it takes <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|00\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">/</mi><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mrow><annotation This leaves the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|00\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> state unchanged, since the control bit is <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> And it takes <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|10\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> to <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>11</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|11\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">1</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> And so the output from the circuit is:</p><p><span><span There’s no obvious interpretation of this state as a classical state, unlike say a computational basis state such as <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|00\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> In fact, entangled states can be used to do all sorts of interesting information processing tasks, including quantum teleportation and fast quantum algorithms.</p><p>A point I glossed over above, but worth mentioning: in the circuit I drew <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> separately as input qubits. It’s conventional to do that kind of thing to denote a combined input of <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|00\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> More generally, people use <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> interchangeably with <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|00\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> interchangeably with <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>01</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|01\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> and so on. Going back and forth takes a bit of getting used to, but everything works pretty much as you expect, and you just need a little practice before it seems quite natural.</p><p>More generally, if we have single-qubit states <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>γ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>δ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\gamma|0\rangle+\delta|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">γ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span together is just:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mtable columnalign="right left" columnspacing="0em" rowspacing="0.24999999999999992em"><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow></mrow></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mpadded depth="0px"><mphantom><mo>=</mo></mphantom></mpadded><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>γ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>δ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mstyle></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow></mrow></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>α</mi><mi>γ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>00</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>α</mi><mi>δ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>01</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi>γ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>β</mi><mi>δ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>11</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mi (\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle)(\gamma|0\rangle+\delta|1\rangle) \\ &amp; = \alpha\gamma|00\rangle+\alpha\delta |01\rangle+ \beta\gamma |10\rangle+ \beta\delta |11\rangle. class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">α</span><span class="mord mathdefault">δ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord mathdefault">γ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">β</span><span class="mord mathdefault">δ</span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mord">.</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>I said that the CNOT leaves the control qubit alone, and modifies the target qubit. That’s true in the computational basis. In fact, it’s actually possible for the target qubit to affect the control qubit. It’s worth taking a minute or two to at least understand (and, if you’re feeling energetic, attempting to solve) the following exercise:</p><p><strong>Exercise:</strong> Can you find single-qubit states <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>a</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|a\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">a</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>b</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|b\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">b</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> so that applying a CNOT to the combined state <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>b</mi><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|ab\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord mathdefault">a</span><span class="mord mathdefault">b</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> changes the first qubit, i.e., the control qubit?</p><p>Let me give you an example which solves the above exercise. Suppose we introduce single-qubit states <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mo>+</mo><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|+\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">+</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mo>−</mo><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|-\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">−</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> defined by:</p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mtable columnalign="right left" columnspacing="0em" rowspacing="0.24999999999999992em"><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mo>+</mo><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>:</mo><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mfrac></mrow></mstyle></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mo>−</mo><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow></mstyle></mtd><mtd><mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>:</mo><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo class=""></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mclose nulldelimiter"></span></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist"><span class=""></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></p><p>A mnemonic for this notation is that these are both equal superpositions of <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> but the “+” or “-” corresponds to the sign of the amplitude for the <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> state. The following circuit identity holds:</p><p><img src="/assets/cnot_backwards.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/cnot_backwards-1x.png, /assets/cnot_backwards.png 2x"></p><p>That is, if we put <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mo>+</mo><mo>−</mo><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|+-\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">−</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> in, then <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mo>−</mo><mo>−</mo><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|--\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mbin">−</span><span class="mspace"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">−</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> comes out, i.e., the target qubit isn’t changed, but the control qubit is! The proof is just to follow the definitions and the algebra through. It’s honestly not terribly enlightening to follow the algebra through at this point – it’s a lot easier once certain other facts are known to you. But, again, if you’re feeling enthusiastic it’s a good exercise to work through to become familiar with how things work.</p><p>It’s worth taking some caution from this example. It means that intuitions coming from the computational basis can sometimes be incomplete or misleading. The CNOT isn’t simply doing something to the target, conditional on the control. It’s also doing something to the control, conditional on the target.</p><div class="exercises"><a name="inverse_CNOT"></a><p><strong>Exercise:</strong> Show that the inverse of the CNOT gate is just the CNOT gate.</p></div><div class="review_set" id="reviewSet-12"><div class="ReviewArea" touch-action="manipulation"><div class="BottomControlArea"><div class="ReviewButtonArea"><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 31 31" fill="none"><path d="M15.5 7C10.2533 7 6 11.2533 6 16.5C6 21.7467 10.2533 26 15.5 26C20.7467 26 25 21.7467 25 16.5C25 15.3037 24.7789 14.159 24.3752 13.1046" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round"></path><path d="M15 11.5V2.5L22 7L15 11.5Z"></path></svg>Didn’t remember</button><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 33 33" fill="none"><path d="M5.99981 18.5564L13.778 26.3345L26.7416 8.18545" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><h1 id="part-III">Part III: Universal quantum computing</h1><blockquote class="Flavor">In natural science, Nature has given us a world and we’re just to discover its laws. In computers, we can stuff laws into it and create a world.<p class="Attribution">— Alan Kay</p></blockquote><p>Let’s return to the question we began with: is there a single computing device that can efficiently simulate <em>any</em> other physical system? At the moment, the best candidate humanity has for such a computing device is a quantum computer. As you’ve probably guessed, you make such a device by combining all the elements we’ve been discussing. In Part III of this essay we’ll discuss what a quantum computer is, why they’re useful, and whether they can be used to efficiently simulate any other physical system.</p><h2 id="the-quantum-computing-model">The quantum computing model</h2><blockquote class="Flavor">The theory of computation has traditionally been studied almost entirely in the abstract, as a topic in pure mathematics. This is to miss the point of it. Computers are physical objects, and computations are physical processes. What computers can or cannot compute is determined by the laws of physics alone, and not by pure mathematics.<p class="Attribution">— David Deutsch</p></blockquote><p>In a general quantum computation, you start out with many qubits – I’ll draw four here, but in general it might be many more (or less). You apply quantum gates of various kinds, in particular, single-qubit gates and CNOT gates. And at the end of the circuit you read out the result by measuring in the computational basis. Here’s what it all looks like:</p><p><img src="/assets/general_circuit.png" class="RetinaImage CropToCenter" srcset="/assets/general_circuit-1x.png, /assets/general_circuit.png 2x"></p><p>I haven’t labeled the single-qubit gates explicitly, but they might be various things – Hadamard gates, NOT gates, rotations, and perhaps others. Note also that while the computation starts in the computational basis state <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0000</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0000\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> you can also start in some other computational basis state. I’ve just chosen <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0000</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0000\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span> for definiteness.</p><p>We can summarize the three steps in a quantum computation as follows:</p><p></p><ol><li>Start in a computational basis state.</li><li>Apply a sequence of CNOT and single-qubit gates.</li><li>To obtain the result, measure in the computational basis. The probability of any result, say <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mn>00</mn><mo>…</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">00\ldots 0</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="minner">…</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord">0</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> is just the square of the absolute value of the corresponding amplitude.</li></ol><p></p><p>That’s all a general quantum computation is! If you understand this model, you know what a quantum computer is. It's pretty simple, really – enough so that I've sometimes heard people say “Is that all there is to it?” But while the model is simple, it contains remarkable depths, and exploring it could occupy many lifetimes.</p><p>In practice, people sometimes introduce other ideas into the way they describe quantum computations. If you’re a programmer, you can think of this as like the way programming language designers introduce higher-level abstractions to help people design different kinds of programs. In principle, those abstractions can always be reduced down to the level of AND and NOT gates. And if you understand that level – AND and NOT, or the type of quantum circuit shown above – then you have a foundation for building an understanding of the other ideas.</p><p>That may leave you wondering: does that mean you <em>also</em> need to master all the higher-level abstractions? The answer is no!</p><p>There are several reasons for this. One reason is that, as we discussed earlier, humanity doesn’t yet know what the higher-level abstractions are. We’re still trying to figure them out. A second reason is that it seems likely that the list of higher-level abstractions is inexhaustible. My guess – and it’s just a guess – is that we will continue to discover beautiful new abstractions <em>forever</em>, in both classical and quantum computing.</p><p>A related idea is that there are models of quantum computation different to the quantum circuit model. Some are merely small variations on the model I’ve described. For instance, instead of only using CNOT gates, we might allow any two-qubit unitary gate to be used in the circuit. Or perhaps instead of using qubits, we might use some other type of basic quantum system – say, the <em>qutrit</em>, which has three computational basis states, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo><mo separator="true">,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|0\rangle, |1\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">0</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span><span class="mpunct">,</span><span class="mspace"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">1</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>,</span> and <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mn>2</mn><mo stretchy="false">⟩</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|2\rangle</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord">2</span><span class="mclose">⟩</span></span></span></span></span>.</span> It probably won’t surprise you that the resulting models of computation are essentially equivalent to the quantum circuit model I’ve described. By this, I mean they can simulate the quantum circuit model (and vice versa) using roughly comparable numbers of gates and other physical resources.</p><p>There are also much more exotic variations, ideas such as measurement-based quantum computation, topological quantum computation, and others. I won’t describe these in any detail here, but suffice to say that they appear superficially very different to the circuit model. Nonetheless, they’re all mathematically equivalent to one another, including to the quantum circuit model. Thus a quantum computation in any of those models can be translated into an equivalent in the quantum circuit model, with only a small overhead in the cost of computation. And vice versa.</p><p>You may wonder why people bother thinking about other models, if they’re mathematically equivalent to the quantum circuit model. The reason is that just because two models are mathematically equivalent doesn’t mean they’re psychologically equivalent. Different models of computation stimulate different ways of thinking, and give rise to different ideas. And so it’s valuable to have other equivalent models.</p><div class="exercises"><a name="product_unitaries"></a><p><strong>Exercise:</strong> We saw earlier that composition of quantum gates corresponds to matrix multiplication (in reverse order). Show that the product of two unitary matrices <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>U</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">U</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">U</span></span></span></span></span> and <span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">V</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">V</span></span></span></span></span> is also unitary. As a consequence, the net effect of any quantum circuit (before measurement) is to effect a unitary operation on the state space of the system.</p></div><div class="review_set" id="reviewSet-13"><div class="ReviewArea" touch-action="manipulation"><div class="BottomControlArea"><div class="ReviewButtonArea"><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 31 31" fill="none"><path d="M15.5 7C10.2533 7 6 11.2533 6 16.5C6 21.7467 10.2533 26 15.5 26C20.7467 26 25 21.7467 25 16.5C25 15.3037 24.7789 14.159 24.3752 13.1046" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round"></path><path d="M15 11.5V2.5L22 7L15 11.5Z"></path></svg>Didn’t remember</button><button class="Button primary regular Disabled" touch-action="manipulation" type="button"><svg viewbox="0 0 33 33" fill="none"><path d="M5.99981 18.5564L13.778 26.3345L26.7416 8.18545" fill="none" stroke-width="4" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"></path></svg>Remembered</button></div></div><p class="CardContainer"></p></div></div><a name="global_phase"></a><p>Let me conclude this section with a brief comment about a particular class of quantum gates. These are gates which are multiples of the identity matrix, <span class="InlineMathContainer"><span><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">I</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut"></span><span class="mord mathdefault">I</span></span></span></span></span>:</span></p><p><span><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math><semantics><mtable columnalign="right"
